147.40013pt\raise 5.39168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{F}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 165.4741pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 165.4741pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-15.17426pt\raise-32.13669pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G\mathbf{BC}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.5912pt\raise-21.46973pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.4014pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{F\mathbf{P}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 165.4741pt\raise-1.23312pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 42.27843pt\raise-32.13669pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 72.27843pt\raise-32.13669pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

We then define
F\mathbf{P}
by left Kan-extension.
Coarsification is also functorial in
F
, i.e., we have actually a functor

(-)\mathbf{P}:{\mathbf{Fun}}(G\mathbf{UBC},{\mathcal{C}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(G\mathbf{BC},{\mathcal{C}})\ .

∎

Coarsification is a tool to produce coarse homology theories.
Recall that
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
is called flasque if it admits a selfmorphism
f:X\to X

such that:

1.

f
is close and homotopic to the identity,

2.

non-expanding in the sense that
there exist a cofinal set of entourages
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}
such that
f(U)\subseteq U
, and

3.

shifting in the sense that for every bounded subset
B
of
X
there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that
f^{n}(X)\cap B=\emptyset
.

Proposition 2.127
.

If
F
is homotopy invariant, excisive for decompositions of simplicial complexes into subcomplexes and vanishes on flasques, then
F\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is an equivariant coarse homology theory.

Proof.

The argument is the same as for
[
BE20a
, Prop. 5.2]
.
The functor
F\mathbf{P}
is
u
-continuous by design.
Homotopy invariance of
F
implies coarse invariance of
F\mathbf{P}
.
Excisiveness of
F
for decompositions of simplicial complexes into subcomplexes implies
excision for
F\mathbf{P}
. Finally, the property of vanishing on flasques is preserved by coarsification.
∎

Remark 2.128
.

The
2.118
of local finiteness can be interpreted for functors on
G\mathbf{UBC}
.
For homotopy invariant functors local finiteness implies vanishing on flasques, see the proof of
[
BE20b
, Lem. 7.21]
.
∎

Let
E:G\mathbf{BornTop}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be some functor and recall the notation
\iota^{\prime}
from (
2.23
).

Definition 2.129
.

We define the coarsification of
E
by

E\mathbf{P}:=(\iota^{\prime,*}E)\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}\ .

(2.149)

Recall that
X
in
G\mathbf{BornTop}
is flasque if it admits a selfmap

f:X\to X
which is homotopic to the identity and shifting in the sense of
Item

3
above.
We then have the analogue of
2.127
:

Proposition 2.130
.

If
E
is homotopy invariant, excisive for decompositions of simplicial complexes into subcomplexes and vanishes on flasques, then
E\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is an equivariant coarse homology theory.

Example 2.131
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{C}}
is a complete and cocomplete stable
\infty
-category and consider a functor
E:G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}\to{\mathcal{C}}
. By
2.122
,
2.120
and
2.128
the functor
E^{\%,{\mathcal{F}},\mathrm{lf}}

satisfies the assumptions of
2.130
and gives rise to an equivariant coarse homology theory

E^{\%,{\mathcal{F}},\mathrm{lf}}\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}\ .

∎

We consider the composition of functors

\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}:G\mathbf{UBC}\xrightarrow{\iota^{\prime}}G\mathbf{BornTop}\xrightarrow{\iota^{\mathrm{top}}}G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\ ,

see (
2.23
) for notation.
Let
E:G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be some functor.

Definition 2.132
.

We define the coarsification of
E
by

E\mathbf{P}:=(\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime,*}E)\mathbf{P}\ .

We have

E\mathbf{P}(X)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}^{G}_{X}}E(\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}P_{U}(X))\ .

(2.150)

Proposition 2.133
.

If
E
is a weak equivariant Borel-Moore homology theory, then
E\mathbf{P}
is a coarse homology theory.

Proof.

We will apply
2.127
to
\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime,*}E
. To this end we verify its assumptions.
It follows from
2.124
that
\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime,*}E
is homotopy invariant and
locally finite. By
2.128
it annihilates flasques.

The functor
\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}
sends decompositions of
G
-simplicial complexes
into
G
-subcomplexes to closed decompositions.
Since
E
is closed excisive,
\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime,*}E
is excisive for such decompositions.
∎

2.15
The coarse character

In this section we introduce the coarse character from equivariant coarse homology theory to
the coarsification of Borel-Moore homology. In a first step we construct on the level of chain complexes a map from coarse homology to the locally finite singular homology of the Rips complex.
We then observe based on the theory of assembly developed in
Section

2.13
that locally finite singular homology on
G\mathbf{BornTop}
has a universal property for mapping out
to other versions of locally finite homology. This will be used in a second step in order to construct a map to the coarsification of the corresponding Borel-Moore homology. Note that Borel-Moore homology as a homology theory on
\mathbf{LCH}^{+}
has a universal property for mapping in, but in the process of coarsification we pull-back the Borel-Moore homology to

\mathbf{BornTop}
using
\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}
, and in this process we loose the corresponding universal property.

Let
k
be a commutative ring and
A
be a
k
-module.
We consider the functor

C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(-,A):G\mathbf{TopBorn}\to{\mathbf{Ch}}_{k}

which sends
X
to the chain complex of
k
-modules of locally finite
G
-invariant singular chains. The elements of
C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X)_{n}
are
G
-invariant functions
\phi:\mathrm{Sing}(X)_{n}\to A
such that
for every bounded subset
B
of
X
the number of simplices of the chain that meet
B
, i.e., the cardinality of the set

\{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sing}(X)_{n}\mid\sigma(\Delta^{n})\cap B\not=\emptyset\wedge\phi(\sigma)\not=0\}\ ,

is finite. The differential
d:C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)_{n}\to C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)_{n-1}
is the usual homological differential. The local finiteness ensures that the sums involved in its definition are finite. Here we use the compatibility of the topology and the bornology which implies that compact subsets are always bounded,
and further that the images of singular simplices are compact and hence bounded.

Recall the notation from (
2.107
). We get the functor

H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A):=\ell C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(-,A):G\mathbf{BornTop}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

(2.151)

The following is standard.

Proposition 2.134
.

The functor

H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
is homotopy invariant, open excisive, and vanishes on flasques.

Recall
2.129
. As a consequence of
2.130
we get:

Corollary 2.135
.

The coarsification

H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)

of
H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
is an equivariant coarse homology theory.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornTop}
and
W
be an invariant
G
-bounded subset. Then the map

C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)\to C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)
is injective. Consequently
we have an equivalence

\ell\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)}\simeq\mathrm{Cofib}(H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X\setminus W,A)\to H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A))\ .

(2.152)

We let

H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A):=(H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A))^{\mathrm{lf}}

be the result of making
H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
locally finite, see
2.119
.
In view
of
(
2.146
) and (
2.152
) we have

H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{W}\ell\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)}\ ,

where
W
runs over the
G
-bounded invariant subsets of
X
.
On the other hand,
the map

C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{W}\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)}

is an isomorphism of chain complexes.
Hence the natural transformation (
2.145
) specializes to

H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)\to H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)

(2.153)

which at
X
is the canonical map

\ell\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{W}\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{W}\ell\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)}\ .

(2.154)

Note the different orders of
\ell
and the limit in domain and target of this map.

Remark 2.136
.

Note that the structure maps

\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)}\to\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W^{\prime},A)}

for
W\subseteq W^{\prime}
are surjective.
If
X
admits a countable exhaustion by
G
-bounded invariant subsets, then
we can apply Mittag-Leffler in order to see that (
2.154
) is an equivalence.
The general case remains unclear so that we do not know whether
H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
is already locally finite.
∎

Recall the equivariant coarse homology with coefficients in
A
from (
2.108
).
We are going to construct a natural
transformation of equivariant coarse homology theories

\kappa^{G}:\mathrm{H}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}(-,A)\to H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

(2.155)

Construction 2.137
.

The transformation in (
2.155
)
will be induced by a chain-level construction.
Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BC}
. For every
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{G}_{X}
containing the diagonal we have
a map

\tilde{\kappa}^{G}_{U}:C{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{U}(X,A)\to C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(P_{U}(X),A)\ ,

(2.156)

where
C{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{U}(X,A)
is the subcomplex of
C{\mathcal{X}}^{G}(X,A)
of
U
-controlled chains.
The map
\tilde{\kappa}^{G}_{U}
sends

\phi
in
C{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{U}(X,A)_{n}
to the chain

\tilde{\kappa}^{G}_{U}(\phi):\mathrm{Sing}_{n}(P_{U}(X))\to A

which sends the singular simplex
\Delta(x_{0},\dots,x_{n}):\Delta^{n}\to P_{U}(X)
to
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
and vanishes elsewhere. Here
\Delta(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
is given in barycentric coordinates by
t=(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{n}t_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, where we interpret points on
P_{U}(X)
as finitely supported probability measures.
Since
\phi
is
U
-controlled the condition
\phi(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\not=0
implies that
\Delta(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
is indeed a well-defined singular simplex in
P_{U}(X)
.
The
G
-invariance or local finiteness of
\phi
implies
G
-invariance or local finiteness of
\tilde{\kappa}^{G}_{U}(\phi)
, respectively.
One checks that
\tilde{\kappa}^{G}_{U}
is a map of chain complexes.

These maps are compatible with the inclusions of chain complexes associated to inclusions of invariant coarse entourages.
Taking the colimit of all
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}
we get a map of chain complexes

\tilde{\kappa}^{G}_{X}:C{\mathcal{X}}^{G}(X,A)\to C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(-,A)\mathbf{P}(X)\ .

The family of maps
(\tilde{\kappa}^{G}_{X})_{X\in G\mathbf{BC}}
is a natural transformation of functors

\tilde{\kappa}^{G}:C{\mathcal{X}}^{G}(-,A)\to C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(-,A)\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Ch}}_{k}\ .

We finally apply
\ell
from (
2.107
) in order to get the transformation
(
2.155
)

∎

We consider the restriction

H_{A}^{G}:G\mathbf{Orb}\xrightarrow{S\mapsto S_{\mathrm{disc}}}G{\mathbf{Top}}\xrightarrow{i}G\mathbf{BornTop}\xrightarrow{H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)}{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)

(2.157)

of
H_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}
to the orbit category, where
i
is as in (
2.141
). Since
H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
is homotopy invariant we have a natural transformation

\mathrm{asmbl}^{H^{G}_{A}}:H_{A}^{G,\%}\to H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A):G\mathbf{BornTop}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

Forcing local finiteness we get a map

\mathrm{asmbl}^{H^{G}_{A},\mathrm{lf}}:H_{A}^{G,\%,\mathrm{lf}}\to H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)\ .

(2.158)

Example 2.138
.

Assume that
T
in
G\mathbf{Orb}
is an infinite orbit.
Since
i(T)
is
T
equipped with the maximal bornology it admits no non-zero
G
-invariant locally finite chains.
Consequently
H_{A}^{G}(T)=0
. Let
S
be an infinite
G
-orbit and
S_{min}
in
G\mathbf{TopBorn}
be
S
equipped with the minimal bornology.
Using the point-wise formula for the left Kan-extension in (
2.139
) we have

H_{A}^{G,\%,\mathrm{lf}}(S_{min})\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{T\in G\mathbf{Orb}_{/S_{min,min}}}H^{G}_{A}(T)\simeq 0

since every
T
in
G\mathbf{Orb}
admitting a non-trivial map to
S
is also infinite.

On the other hand we have

\pi_{0}H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(S_{min},A)\cong\pi_{0}H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(S_{min},A)\cong A\ .

Hence the assembly map is not an equivalence for infinite groups.
∎

Proposition 2.139
.

If
G
is finite and
|G|
is invertible in
k
, then the assembly map (
2.158
) is an equivalence.

Proof.

Let

C^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A):G\mathbf{BornTop}\to{\mathbf{Ch}}_{k}

be the functor which sends
X
in
G\mathbf{BornTop}
to the chain complex of finite
G
-invariant
A
-valued singular chains. We further define

H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A):=\ell C^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A):G\mathbf{BornTop}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

Lemma 2.140
.

If
G
is finite and
|G|
is invertible in
k
, then

H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
is an equivariant homology theory on
G{\mathbf{Top}}
.

Proof.

We use the isomorphism
C^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A)\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A)
. Since
|G|
is invertible in
k

we have equivalences
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}\ell\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}\ell\simeq\ell\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}
of functors from
BG
to
{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)
, where
\ell:{\mathbf{Ch}}_{k}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)
. Consequently,

H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}H_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A)\ .

We now use that
H_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
is a non-equivariant homology theory and corresponds to a colimit-preserving functor from
\mathbf{Spc}
to
{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)
denoted by the same symbol.
We can therefore write

H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
as the composition

G{\mathbf{Top}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Y^{G}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{PSh}}(G\mathbf{Orb})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{G}^{*}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathbf{Spc})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ ,

where
j_{G}:BG\to G\mathbf{Orb}
is the inclusion.
It follows that

H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)
is the equivariant homology theory represented by the functor

j_{G,!}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}H_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A):G\mathbf{Orb}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

∎

It follows from
2.140
that the assembly map

H^{G,\%}_{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)

is an equivalence.

Since
G
is finite the condition of being
G
-bounded is equivalent to being bounded.
Let
W
be a
G
-bounded invariant subset of
X
.
Then we have an isomorphism of chain complexes

\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G}(X\setminus W,A)}\to\frac{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A)}{C_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X\setminus W,A)}

induced by the canonical inclusions.
Hence for every
G
bounded invariant subset
W
we get an equivalence

\mathrm{Cofib}(H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X\setminus W,A)\to H_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G}(X,A))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Cofib}(H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X\setminus W,A)\to H_{{\mathtt{sing}}}^{G,\mathrm{LF}}(X,A))\ .

Applying
\ell
and taking the limit we get an equivalence

H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A)^{\mathrm{lf}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(X,A):G\mathbf{BornTop}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

Composing these equivalences we get the desired equivalence

H_{A}^{G,\%,\mathrm{lf}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}H^{G}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)^{\mathrm{lf}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)\ .

∎

We assume that
G
is finite and that
|G|
is invertible in
K
.

Definition 2.141
.

The locally finite coarse character is the natural transformation

\chi^{G,\mathrm{lf}}:\mathrm{H}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}(-,A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\kappa^{G},\eqref{rgrwtbwergwerfwerf}}}{{\to}}H^{G,\mathrm{LF}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)\mathbf{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{hfgfqzwegfuzfqewf}}}{{\to}}H^{G,\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathtt{sing}}}(-,A)\mathbf{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq,\eqref{porjgoiergwerfwerfref}}}{{\leftarrow}}H_{A}^{G,\%,\mathrm{lf}}\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

(2.159)

In view of
2.138
and the presence of the inverted map in (
2.159
)
the locally finite coarse character is only defined for finite groups
G
with
|G|
invertible in
k
.

In the case of the trivial group
G
, by
2.104
we can associate to every spectrum
F
a Borel-Moore homology theory
F_{\mathrm{BM}}
. We use this in order to construct the coarse Borel-Moore character.

Definition 2.142
.

The coarse Borel-Moore character is defined as the natural transformation

\chi_{\mathrm{BM}}:\mathrm{H}{\mathcal{X}}(-,A)\xrightarrow{\chi^{\mathrm{lf}},\eqref{gwergwergwrfregw}}HA^{\%,\mathrm{lf}}\mathbf{P}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{asmbl}^{\iota^{\mathrm{top},*}HA_{\mathrm{BM}},\mathrm{lf}},\eqref{fervjoierjveoijmewoiwmo}}HA_{\mathrm{BM}}\mathbf{P}:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ .

(2.160)

Definition 2.143
.

We define the periodic Borel-Moore character as the composition

P(\chi_{\mathrm{BM}}):\mathrm{PH}{\mathcal{X}}(-,A)\xrightarrow{P(\chi_{BM})}P(HA_{\mathrm{BM}}\mathbf{P})\to(\prod_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Sigma^{2k}HA_{\mathrm{BM}})\mathbf{P}:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)\ ,

(2.161)

The second map in (
2.161
) is the canonical one induced by the projections

P(HA_{\mathrm{BM}}\mathbf{P})\to\Sigma^{2k}HA_{\mathrm{BM}}\mathbf{P}
for all
k
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We consider (
2.161
) as a transformation of functors from

BG\times\mathbf{BC}
to
{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)
such that
G
-acts trivially. Specializing (
2.20
)
it naturally induces the Borel-equivariant version

P(\chi_{\mathrm{BM}})^{hG}:\mathrm{PH}{\mathcal{X}}^{hG}(-,A)\to((\prod_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Sigma^{2k}HA_{\mathrm{BM}})\mathbf{P})^{hG}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(Hk)

(2.162)

of the periodic Borel-Moore character.

2.16
The topological transgression

In this section we construct for any weak equivariant Borel-Moore homology theory
E^{G}:G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\to{\mathcal{C}}
the topological transgression
as a natural transformation

T^{G,\mathrm{top}}:E^{G}\mathbf{P}\to\Sigma E^{G}\circ\partial_{h}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}\ .

(2.163)

It is a homotopy theoretic generalization to the context of all of
G\mathbf{BC}

of the transgression defined in
[
Wul22
]
.

Assume that
X
is in
G\mathbf{BC}
and let
U
be an invariant coarse entourage of
X
containing the diagonal.
We consider
(X,U)
as an object of
G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}
from
2.11
.
By
[
BL24
, Lem. 3.6]
(see
2.23
)
we can define the Higson corona
\partial_{h}P_{U}(X)
of the metric Rips complex using continuous functions. We have an exact sequence

0\to C_{0}(P_{U}(X))\to C_{{\mathcal{B}}_{P_{U}(X)}}(P_{U}(X))\to C(\partial_{h}P_{U}(X))\to 0

(2.164)

of commutative
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras. Recall that
X_{U}
in
G\mathbf{BC}
denotes the underlying
G
-bornological space of
X
equipped with the
G
-coarse structure generated by
U
.
The inclusion
X_{U}\to P_{U}(X)
as the zero skeleton is equivariant and becomes a coarse equivalence after forgetting the
G
-action. By
2.2
we therefore get an isomorphism

\partial_{h}X_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\partial_{h}P_{U}(X)

of Higson coronas
in
G\mathbf{CH}
.
This isomorphism is the component at
(X,U)
of a natural isomorphism
of functors

\partial_{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\partial_{h}P_{-}(-):G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}\to G\mathbf{CH}\ .

The exact sequence (
2.164
) presents an equivariant compactification

\overline{\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}(P_{U}(X))}
of
\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}(P_{U}(X))
by
\partial_{h}X_{U}
.
A morphism
(X,U)\to(X^{\prime},U^{\prime})
in
G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}
induces a map of pairs

(\overline{\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}(P_{U}(X))},\partial_{h}X_{U})\to(\overline{\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}(P_{U^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}))},\partial_{h}X^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}})

in
G\mathbf{CH}
.
We thus have a functor

G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}\to G\mathbf{CH}^{(2)}\ ,\qquad(X,U)\mapsto(\overline{\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}(P_{U}(X))},\partial_{h}X_{U})\ ,

where
G\mathbf{CH}^{(2)}
denotes the category of pairs in
G\mathbf{CH}
.

Assume now that
E^{G}:G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is a weak equivariant Borel-Moore homology theory.
Using the notation (
2.126
) it induces the functor

E^{G}(-,-):G\mathbf{CH}^{(2)}\to{\mathcal{C}}\ ,\qquad(X,Y)\mapsto E^{G}(X,Y)\ .

We have the functor

(-_{2}):G\mathbf{CH}^{(2)}\to G\mathbf{CH}\ ,\quad(X,Y)\mapsto Y

and a natural transformation

\partial:E^{G}(-,-)\to\Sigma E^{G}(-_{2}):G\mathbf{CH}^{(2)}\to{\mathcal{C}}

which for
(X,Y)
in
G\mathbf{CH}^{(2)}
is the boundary map

E^{G}(X,Y)\to\Sigma E^{G}(Y)
.

We define a natural transformation
of functors

E^{G}(\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}P_{-}(-))\to\Sigma E\circ\partial_{h}:G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}

(2.165)

whose component at
(X,U)
in
G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}
is the composition

E^{G}(\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}P_{U}(X))\simeq E^{G}(\overline{\iota^{\mathrm{top},\prime}(P_{U}(X))},\partial_{h}X_{U})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\to}}\Sigma E^{G}(\partial_{h}X_{U})\to\Sigma E^{G}(\partial_{h}X)\ ,

(2.166)

where we use the strong excision property of
E^{G}
for the first equivalence and the last map is induced by the canonical map
X_{U}\to X
.

Definition 2.144
.

We define the topological transgression

T^{G,\mathrm{top}}:E^{G}\mathbf{P}\to\Sigma E^{G}\circ\partial_{h}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}

as the left Kan-extension of (
2.165
)
indicated in

\textstyle{G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma E^{G}\partial_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\Uparrow\eqref{fvwerpojvofvdsvdfvds}}
\scriptstyle{E(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}P_{-}(-))}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\mathbf{BC}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma E^{G}\partial_{h}}
\scriptstyle{E^{G}\mathbf{P}}
\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{top}}\Uparrow}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}

(2.167)

We refer to
2.126
for the justification of the domain.
In order to see that we get the correct target we use that
the target of (
2.165
) is pulled back along the forgetful functor
G\mathbf{BC}^{{\mathcal{C}}}\to G\mathbf{BC}
.

In our main diagram (
1.2
) we need the Borel-equivariant version of the topological transgression

(T^{\mathrm{top}})^{hG}:(E\mathbf{P})^{hG}\to\Sigma E^{hG}\circ\partial_{h}\ .

(2.168)

for a functor

E
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,{\mathbf{Fun}}_{\mathrm{BM}}(\mathbf{CH},{\mathcal{C}})
.
It is defined as the composition

(E\mathbf{P})^{hG}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{\simeq}}(\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}E\mathbf{P})^{u}\xrightarrow{(\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}T^{\mathrm{top}})^{u}}(\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}\Sigma E\circ\partial_{h})^{u}\to\Sigma E^{hG}\circ\partial_{h}\ .

The last map is induced by the canonical maps

\displaystyle(\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}\Sigma E\circ\partial_{h})^{u}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}\Sigma E(\partial_{h}X_{U})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{can}}}{{\to}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}}\Sigma E(\partial_{h}X_{U})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{can}}}{{\to}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{BG}\Sigma E(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}}\partial_{h}X_{U})\to\Sigma E^{hG}(\partial_{h}X)

where the last map is induced by the canonical map (in fact an isomorphism)

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}}\partial_{h}X_{U}\to\partial_{h}X

in
G\mathbf{CH}
.

Remark 2.145
.

Let
E^{G}:G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be a weak equivariant Borel-Moore homology theory.
Then by
2.9
and similarly as in
2.47
we can construct a factorization

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.27267pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise R(E^{G}\partial_{h})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 95.1953pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.27267pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 32.85013pt\raise-26.69502pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.21724pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{top}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.99664pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 72.05467pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 38.27267pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 75.99664pt\raise-32.91226pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma E^{G}\partial_{h}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

The dotted arrow is the best approximation of the topological transgression
by a natural transformation between equivariant coarse homology theories.
Non-vanishing of
T^{G,\mathrm{top}}
implies non-vanishing of
R(E^{G}\partial_{h})
and of the dotted arrow.

Consider, e.g., the example of a trivial group and
X={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
in
\mathbf{BC}
with the metric structures. Then we have a canonical morphism
q:\partial_{h}{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to S^{n-1}
. Since
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is coarsifying (see
[
BE20a
, Def. 7.8 and Prop. 7.13]
)
we have
\Sigma^{n}E(*)\simeq E({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\simeq E\mathbf{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and
the composition

\Sigma^{n}E(*)\simeq E\mathbf{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T^{\mathrm{top}}}}{{\to}}\Sigma E(\partial_{h}{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\to}}\Sigma E(S^{n-1})\to\Sigma^{n}E(*)\oplus\Sigma E(*)

(2.169)

is the inclusion of the first summand.
∎

3
Construction of fillers

3.1
Cone functors

In this subsection we recall the definitions of the cone functors

{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{BC}

and the cone sequence.
We refer to
[
BE20b
, Sec. 5.3.3]
,
[
BE20a
, Sec. 8]
for the non-equivariant, and to
[
BEKW20d
, Sec. 9.4]
for the equivariant case. As there are slightly varying conventions how to treat the time variable we give complete definitions here.

Let
\iota:G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{BC}
be the forgetful functor. We first describe the functor
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}
. For
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}

the cone
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
in
G\mathbf{BC}
is obtained from
\iota({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X)
in
G\mathbf{BC}
by taking the new
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)}
consisting of all coarse entourages
U
of
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
with the additional property that for every uniform entourage

V
of
X
there exists
s
in
{\mathbb{R}}
such that

U\cap(\{t\}\times X\})\times(\{t\}\times X\})\subseteq V
for every
t
in
[s,\infty)
.
If
f:X\to X^{\prime}
is a morphism in
G\mathbf{UBC}
, then the morphism
\iota(\mathrm{id}\otimes f):\iota({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X)\to\iota({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X^{\prime})

in
G\mathbf{BC}
functorially induces a morphism
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(f):{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X^{\prime})
.

The cone
{\mathcal{O}}(X)
in
G\mathbf{BC}
is defined as the subspace

{\mathcal{O}}(X):=\{(t,x)\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\mid t\geq 0\}

(3.1)

of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
with the induced structures.

Definition 3.1
.

The functors
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty},{\mathcal{O}}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{BC}
described above are called the (geometric) cone-at-
\infty
functor and the cone functor.

For
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
we have an inclusion

\iota X\to{\mathcal{O}}(X)
,
x\mapsto(0,x)
. The cone boundary

\partial^{\mathrm{cone}}:{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\to{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X

is given by the identity of underlying
G
-sets.
The sequence

\iota X\to{\mathcal{O}}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{\mathrm{cone}}}}{{\to}}\iota({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X)

is called the geometric cone sequence.
Applying
\mathrm{Yo}^{G}
from (
2.5
) and using
\mathrm{Yo}^{G}\iota({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X)\simeq\Sigma\mathrm{Yo}^{G}\iota X
we get a fibre sequence

\mathrm{Yo}^{G}\iota X\to\mathrm{Yo}^{G}{\mathcal{O}}(X)\to\mathrm{Yo}^{G}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\to\Sigma\mathrm{Yo}^{G}\iota X

(3.2)

which is also called the cone sequence
[
BEKW20d
, Cor. 9.30]
.
The fibre sequence (
3.2
) shows that

\mathrm{Yo}^{G}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Cofib}(\mathrm{Yo}^{G}\iota X\to\mathrm{Yo}^{G}{\mathcal{O}}(X))
which motivates the name
”cone-at-
\infty
” for
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}
.

If
S
is a
G
-set, then

{\mathcal{O}}(S_{min,min,disc})\cong[0,\infty)\otimes S_{min,min}
is flasque. Hence in view of the fibre sequence (
3.2
)
the cone boundary induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Yo}^{G}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(S_{min,min,disc})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\Sigma\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(S_{min,min})\ ,

(3.3)

see also
[
BEKW20d
, Prop.9.35]
.

Remark 3.2
.

If
E:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is a strong coarse homology theory, then the compositions of
E
with the cone functors
are equivariant local homology theories

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty},E{\mathcal{O}}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}\ ,

see
[
BE20a
, Lem. 9.2]
for the non-equivariant case, and also
Section

3.8
. They are in particular homotopy invariant, annihilate flasques and are
excisive for decompositions of simplicial complexes. So by
2.127
they can serve as an input for the coarsification.

In the non-equivariant case, the functor
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}
is the domain of the coarse assembly map whose fibre is represented by
E{\mathcal{O}}\mathbf{P}
, see
[
BE20a
, Sec. 9]
.

∎

3.2
Normalization at a point

This section provides our starting point for the stepwise construction of fillers of our squares. We show that the homotopy Chern character
can be normalized such that the diagram (
1.13
) commutes when we insert

Z=*
.

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists a unique choice of the equivalence (
1.9
) used in the construction of
{\mathbf{ch}}^{h}
such that the big cell in the diagram (
1.13
) commutes at
Z=*
.

Proof.

We evaluate the diagram (
1.13
) at
Z=*
. In the lower right corner we have

\Sigma(\prod_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Sigma^{2k}H{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{BM}})(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(*))\simeq\prod_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}\ .

If
F
is any spectrum, then the datum of a map

f:F\to\prod_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}
is equivalent to a family
({\mathtt{pr}}_{k}\circ f)_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
of maps
{\mathtt{pr}}_{k}\circ f:F\to\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}
. For each
k

such a map is in turn uniquely determined up to equivalence by a map

f_{k}:\pi_{2k+1}F\to\pi_{2k+1}\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
.

By (
3.3
)
the cone boundary induces an equivalence

\partial^{\mathrm{cone}}:\mathrm{Yo}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\Sigma\mathrm{Yo}(*)\ .

(3.4)

Hence
\mathrm{Yo}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*))\in\mathbf{Sp}{\mathcal{X}}\langle*\rangle
and the comparison map
c
from
2.50
is an equivalence at

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*)
by

2.51
.
The upper left corner of (
1.13
) at
Z={\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Z)
can therefore be calculated
using the equivalence

K{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\simeq}}K{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{\mathrm{cone}},\eqref{gijoergrtg}}}{{\simeq}}\Sigma K{\mathcal{X}}(*)\simeq KU

and

\pi_{2k+1}\Sigma KU={\mathbb{Z}}
for every
k
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
.

In view of these calculations
the transformations
\phi
and
\psi
are given by families
(\phi_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}},(\psi_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}

of maps
\phi_{k},\psi_{k}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
.
Thereby
\phi
depends on the choice of the equivalence (
1.9
) and
\psi
does not.

We must show that we can normalize (
1.9
) and therefore
{\mathbf{ch}}^{h}
uniquely such that

\phi_{k}=\psi_{k}
for all
k
in
{\mathbb{Z}}
. The map
\phi_{k}
depends homogeneously on
the component

{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\pi_{2k+1}\Sigma KU\to\pi_{2k+1}\Sigma KU\wedge H{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{vdsfpovkspdfvfdvsf}}}{{\simeq}}\pi_{2k+1}\prod_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{pr}}_{k}}}{{\to}}\pi_{2k+1}\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}\cong{\mathbb{C}}

of (
1.9
).
If we fix one choice and show that the resulting
\phi_{k}
is not zero, then there is a unique normalization of this component so that
\phi_{k}=\psi_{k}
.

The following lemma thus finishes the proof of
3.3
.

Lemma 3.4
.

The component
\phi_{k}
is non-zero.

Proof.

By
Theorem

3.12
below we have a commutative triangle

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.757pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.757pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.16983pt\raise 6.26584pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.90474pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p^{\mathrm{an}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 156.38683pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.17209pt\raise-10.52332pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T^{\mathrm{an}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.97035pt\raise-24.21724pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 90.07191pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise K^{\mathrm{an}}(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(*))}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-32.21724pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 53.757pt\raise-32.21724pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma K^{\mathrm{an}}(\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 134.48283pt\raise-11.60168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.02116pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 187.11491pt\raise-32.21724pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

Note that the map
T^{\mathrm{mot}}
in (
3.14
) is the identity when
X={\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
as in the our present situation. By
[
BELa
, Thm. 1.5]
the morphism
p^{\mathrm{an}}
is an equivalence. Therefore
\pi\circ T^{\mathrm{an}}
is an equivalence, too. The component of
{\mathbf{ch}}^{h}
on
*
is precisely

\Sigma K^{\mathrm{an}}(*)\simeq\Sigma KU\to\Sigma KU\wedge H{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{vdsfpovkspdfvfdvsf}}}{{\simeq}}\prod_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}\ .

It follows that

\phi_{k}:{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\pi_{2k+1}K{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*))\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{pr}}_{k}\circ{\mathbf{ch}}^{h}\circ\pi\circ T^{\mathrm{an}}\circ c}\pi_{2k+1}\Sigma^{2k+1}H{\mathbb{C}}\cong{\mathbb{C}}

is injective, in particular non-zero.
∎

The
3.4
finishes the proof of
3.3

∎

3.3
The motivic transgression

In this section we introduce the concept of the motivic transgression.
It will be used to extend the commutativity of the diagram (
1.10
) from the case where

X
is a cone over some uniform bornological coarse space
Y
to the situation where
X
only contains
such a cone coarsely. We furthermore describe the corresponding projection map on the level of Higson coronas.

We start with recalling some basics about coarsely excisive pairs.
Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BC}
.
According to
[
BEKW20d
, Def. 3.3]
a subset
C
of
X
is nice if the inclusion
C\to U[C]
is a coarse equivalence for every invariant coarse entourage
U
of
X
containing the diagonal.
Let
A,B
be invariant subspaces of
X
such that
A\cup B=X
.
The following is the corrected version of

[
BEKW20d
, Def. 4.13]
as stated in
[
BKW21
, Def. 2.41]
.

Definition 3.5
.

The pair
(A,B)
is coarsely excisive, if

A,A\cap B
are nice in
X
and
V[A]\cap B
is nice in
X
for a cofinal family of invariant entourages
V
of
X
, and for every coarse entourage
U
of
X
there exists another coarse entourage
V
such that

U[A]\cap U[B]\subseteq V[A\cap B]
.

Remark 3.6
.

The point of
3.5
is that it implies for a coarsely excisive pair
(A,B)
that the square

\textstyle{\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(A\cap B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(X)}

is a push-out in
G\mathbf{Sp}{\mathcal{X}}
.
2
2

2

The conditions stated in
[
BEKW20d
, Def. 4.13]
are not sufficient to get this conclusion as stated in
[
BEKW20d
, Cor. 4.14]
.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

If

\textstyle{A\cap B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}

is a push-out in
G\mathbf{BC}
,
A
and
A\cap B
are nice in
X
, and

V[A]\cap B
is nice in
X
for a cofinal family of invariant entourages
V
of
X
, then the pair

(A,B)
is coarsely excisive.

Proof.

We must verify the last condition stated in
3.5
.
We define a coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
on
X
as follows:
An entourage
U
of
X
belongs to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
if
there exist coarse entourages
V
of
A
and
W
of
B
such that

U\subseteq V\circ W\cup W\circ V
.
We have
{\mathtt{diag}}(X)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is closed under taking subsets.
We have

U^{-1}\subseteq W^{-1}\circ V^{-1}\cup V^{-1}\circ W^{-1}
so that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is closed under the flip.
If
U^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}\circ W^{\prime}\cup W^{\prime}\circ V^{\prime}
, then

U\circ U^{\prime}\subseteq V\circ W\circ V^{\prime}\circ W^{\prime}\cup V\circ W\circ W^{\prime}\circ V^{\prime}\cup W\circ V\circ V^{\prime}\circ W^{\prime}\cup W\circ V\circ W^{\prime}\circ V^{\prime}\ .

The restriction of
W\circ V^{\prime}
to
A\cap B
is an entourage of
A\cap B
.
Hence we have
V\circ W\circ V^{\prime}\circ W^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime\prime}\circ W^{\prime\prime}
for suitable
V^{\prime\prime}
and
W^{\prime\prime}
.
A similar reasoning applies to the other terms.
We conclude that
U\circ U^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
. Finally,

U\cup U^{\prime}\subseteq(V\cup V^{\prime})\circ(W\cup W^{\prime})\cup(W\cup W^{\prime})\circ(V\cup V^{\prime})\ .

We conclude that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is a coarse structure.

We have
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
contains the generators of
{\mathcal{C}}
. It follows that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{C}}
.
We now observe that

U[A]\cap U[B]\subseteq(V\cup W)[A\cap B]\subseteq(V\circ W\cup W\circ V)[A\cap B]\ ,

provided

V
and
W
contain the respective diagonals.
∎

We consider
X^{\prime}
in
G\mathbf{BC}
and
Y
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
. We form the cone

{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
in
G\mathbf{BC}
according to
3.1
, and further assume a coarse subspace inclusion

{\mathcal{O}}(Y)\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
. We let
Z:=X^{\prime}\setminus(0,\infty)\times Y
denote the complement of this cone.
We then define
\hat{X}^{\prime}
in
G\mathbf{BC}
by the push-out

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 30.72795pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-30.72795pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(-\infty,0]\otimes\iota Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.72795pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 54.72795pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 73.75577pt\raise-16.00446pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.75577pt\raise-23.99109pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-28.71521pt\raise-32.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(-\infty,0]\otimes 66.57521pt\raise-32.00891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern (3.5)

We have a canonical inclusion
i:X^{\prime}\to\hat{X}^{\prime}
which identifies
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
as a subspace of
X^{\prime}
with the positive part of the cone
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
and the subspace
Z
of
X^{\prime}
with
\{0\}\times Z
.

Lemma 3.8
.

The decomposition
((-\infty,0]\otimes Z,{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))
of
\hat{X}^{\prime}
is coarsely excisive.

Proof.

We want to apply
3.7
for
A=(-\infty,0]\otimes Z)
and
B={\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
.
To this end it suffices to check that
V[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z)]\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
is nice
in
\hat{X}
for every invariant coarse entourage
V
of
\hat{X}^{\prime}
, and that

(-\infty,0]\otimes Z
is nice in
X
. The case of
V={\mathtt{diag}}(X)
then settles niceness of
(-\infty,0]\otimes Z)\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
.

For the first condition must show that
for any invariant coarse entourage

U
of
\hat{X}^{\prime}
the map

V[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)\to U[V[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)]

is an equivariant coarse equivalence.
The complement of
V[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
in
U[V[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)]
consists of points
(t,y)
in
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
with
0<t<c
for some
c
only depending on
U
and
V
.
An equivariant coarse inverse of the inclusion is given by
the map
which is the identity on
V[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
and sends the point
(t,y)
in the complement of this set to
(0,y)

We now show that
(-\infty,0]\otimes Z
is nice in
X
. Let
U
be an invariant coarse entourage of
X
.
We must show that

(-\infty,0]\otimes Z\to U[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]
is an equivariant coarse equivalence.
We define a coarse inverse
U[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]\to(-\infty,0]\otimes Z
as follows.
This map is the identity on
(-\infty,0]\otimes Z
. Any point in
U[(-\infty,0]\otimes Z]\setminus(-\infty,0]\otimes Z

is of the form
(t,y)
for
t\in[0,c]
with
c
only depending on
U
. Our map sends this point to
(0,y)
in
(-\infty,0]\otimes Z
.
∎

The
flasqueness of the spaces on the left column of (
3.5
) shows that the inclusion
j
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(j):\mathrm{Yo}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(\hat{X}^{\prime})\ .

(3.6)

The map

\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(X^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(\hat{X}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j,\simeq}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathrm{Yo}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))

(3.7)

in
G\mathbf{Sp}{\mathcal{X}}
is a special case of the motivic transgresion.

We now generalize the geometric situation.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)\to X^{\prime}
be as above,

X
be in
G\mathbf{BC}
, and
\psi:X\to X^{\prime}
be a morphism in

G\mathbf{BC}
.

Definition 3.9
.

In this situation we say that the motivic transgression

T^{\mathrm{mot}}:\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(X)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Yo}(\psi)}\mathrm{Yo}^{G}(X^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\eqref{gwerfwerfrwerfref234}}\mathrm{Yo}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\ .

(3.8)

is defined.

The construction of the motivic transgression is complemented by a projection maps on the level of Higson coronas which we describe now.
In
X^{\prime}
we consider
the invariant big family

{\mathcal{Y}}
generated by the bounded subsets of
\{0\}\times Y
. Note that
{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}
, where
{\mathcal{B}}
denotes the bornology of
X^{\prime}
.
We define a homomorphism of commutative
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras

C_{u,0}(Y)\to\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(X^{\prime})\ ,\quad\phi\mapsto\tilde{\phi}\ ,

(3.9)

where
\tilde{\phi}(t,y):=\phi(y)
for
(t,y)
in
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
and
\phi(x)=0
for
x\in Z

(the justification is similar is
[
BELa
, Lem. 5.7]
).
This gives a homomorphism of commutative
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras

C_{u,0}(Y)\to\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(X^{\prime})/\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}})\to\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(X^{\prime})/\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})=C(\partial_{h}X^{\prime})\ .

Its Gelfand dual is a map

\partial_{h}X^{\prime}\to\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(Y)\ .

(3.10)

Definition 3.10
.

If the motivic transgression (
3.8
) is defined, then we define the map

k:\partial_{h}X\xrightarrow{\partial_{h}\psi}\partial_{h}X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\eqref{gwerfwerfwerf252}}\iota_{u}^{\mathrm{top}}(Y)\ .

(3.11)

Example 3.11
.

Let
Y
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}
.
For
X=X^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(Y)
the motivic transgression

T^{\mathrm{mot}}:\mathrm{Yo}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))\to\mathrm{Yo}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))

is defined and induced by the canonical inclusion
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
.
In this case the map
k
is given by the composition

k:\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}(Y)\to\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ewrferfrfvsdfvfd}}}{{\to}}\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(Y)\ .

(3.12)

∎

3.4
The analytic Paschke morphism

The following theorem compares the
K
-theoretic transgression introduced in
2.43
with the
analytic Paschke transformation

p^{G,\mathrm{an}}:K{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{{\mathbf{C}},A,G_{can,max}}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(-)\to K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}\circ\iota_{u}^{\mathrm{top}}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)

(3.13)

from
[
BELa
, Thm. 1.5 & Def. 6.3]
.
We assume that the motivic transgression is defined according to
3.9
.

Theorem 3.12
.

We have a commutative square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 57.16837pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-45.67526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.0466pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T^{\mathrm{mot}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.93431pt\raise 6.21724pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.21724pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{an}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.07466pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.07466pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Sigma K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}(\partial_{h}X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.65529pt\raise-16.42836pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{k,\eqref{ferfjwerfmerfireojoijo}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.65529pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-57.16837pt\raise-32.85672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{{\mathbf{C}},A,G_{can,max}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.6137pt\raise-25.95892pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5367pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p^{G,\mathrm{an}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.16837pt\raise-32.85672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 81.16837pt\raise-32.85672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}(\iota_{u}^{\mathrm{top}}(Y))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(3.14)

Proof.

Both maps
k\circ T^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{X}
and
p_{Y}^{G,\mathrm{an}}\circ T^{\mathrm{mot}}
are induced by the composition of multiplication maps of
C^{*}
-categories

\mu,\mu^{\prime}:C_{u,0}(Y)\otimes{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(X)\to\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}

with the diagonal
\delta_{Y}
similar as in (
2.56
).
The result will follow from a comparison of these multiplication maps.

We first describe
\mu
by unfolding the definitions. It sends the object
(C,\rho,\nu)
of
C_{u,0}(Y)\otimes{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(X)
to the object
(C,\rho)
in
\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}
and the morphism
\phi\otimes A
in
C_{u,0}(Y)\otimes{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(X)
to the morphism
[\tilde{\phi}A]
in
\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}
,
where
[\tilde{\phi}A]
is an abbreviation for

[\nu(\psi^{*}\tilde{\phi})A]
, see (
3.9
) and
(
2.55
) for notation.

Unfolding
3.9
the map
T^{\mathrm{mot}}
is induced by the functor

{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi_{*}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\hat{X}^{\prime})\to{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\hat{X}^{\prime})/{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\{(-\infty,0]\times Z\}\subseteq\hat{X}^{\prime})

(3.15)

followed by an inverse of the unitary equivalence induced by
j
in (
3.5
)

{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))/{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\{(-\infty,0]\times Y\}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\to{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\hat{X}^{\prime})/{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\{(-\infty,0]\times Z\}\subseteq\hat{X}^{\prime})

(see (
3.6
) for the motivic version).
For the inverse we choose a functor
which sends the object
(C,\rho,\nu^{\prime})
in
{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\hat{X}^{\prime})
to the object
(\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))C,\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\rho,\nu^{\prime}_{|{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)})

and the morphism
[A]
in
{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\hat{X}^{\prime})/{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\{(-\infty,0]\times Z\}\subseteq\hat{X}^{\prime})
to
[\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))A]
in
{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))/{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(\{(-\infty,0]\times Y\}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))
. Implicitly we have chosen images of the projections
\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))
.

The composition (
3.15
) sends
(C,\rho,\nu)
in
{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(X)
to

(\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))C,\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))\rho,\nu_{|{\mathcal{O}}(Y)})
and

A
to
[\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))A]
, where
\nu^{\prime}:=\psi_{*}\nu
.

The morphism
\mu^{\prime}
is now given by the functor which sends

(C,\rho,\nu)
to
(\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))C,\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))\rho)
and

\phi\otimes A
to
[\tilde{\phi}\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))A]
.

Since
\tilde{\phi}A=\tilde{\phi}\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))A
we see that

\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are MvN equivalent. This MvN-equivalence is implemented by the family of isometries

v_{(C,\rho,\nu)}:\nu^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}(Y))C\to C
. Since

\mathrm{e}(\mu)\simeq\mathrm{e}(\mu^{\prime}):\mathbf{E}^{G}(C_{u,0}(Y),C_{u,0}(Y)\otimes{\mathbf{V}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G}(X)\otimes A)\to\mathbf{E}^{G}(C_{u,0}(Y),\mathbf{Q}^{G}_{\mathrm{std}}\otimes A)

we conclude that

k\circ T^{\mathrm{an}}_{X}\simeq\mu\circ\delta_{Y}\simeq\mu^{\prime}\circ\delta_{Y}\simeq p^{\mathrm{an}}_{Y}\circ T^{\mathrm{mot}}\ .

∎

The commutative square in (
3.14
) has the draw-back that the right lower corner
is a locally finite functor in the variable
Y
while the left lower corner is not known to be locally finite. So we do not expect that the lower horizontal map
p^{G}_{Y,\mathrm{an}}
is an equivalence for non-compact
Y
in general. In the following we improve this point.

To any functor
E:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
to a stable and complete target we functorially associate a locally finite version

E^{\mathrm{lf}}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
and a natural transformation

E\to E^{\mathrm{lf}}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}

(3.16)

(in analogy to (
2.145
)) such that

E^{\mathrm{lf}}(Z):=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{W}\mathrm{Cofib}(E(Z\setminus W)\to E(Z))\ ,

where
W
runs over all invariant
G
-bounded subsets of
Z
(see (
2.146
)).
This construction preserves homotopy invariance and excisiveness by the analogue of
2.121
.
Following the conventions from
[
BELa
]
we abbreviate

\Sigma K^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}(-):=K{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{{\mathbf{C}},A,G_{can,max}}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(-):G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)\ .

Since
K^{G,\mathrm{an},}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}
is a Borel-Moore homology theory by
2.98
and therefore a weak Borel-Moore homology theory by
2.96
it follows from the
\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}
version
of
2.124
(with the same proof) that
K^{G,\mathrm{an},}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}\circ\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}
is already locally finite.
We can extend the diagram in
Theorem

3.12
as follows:

34.1772pt\raise-42.46227pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p_{Y}^{G,\mathrm{an},\mathrm{lf}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.46605pt\raise-41.27336pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 106.16219pt\raise-65.8634pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

The Paschke transformation is a natural transformation between
functors which are homotopy invariant and excisive for cell attachements.
By
[
BELa
, Thm. 1.5]
it is an equivalence on objects in
G\mathbf{UBC}
of the form
S_{min,min}
for

S
in
G_{\mathbf{Fin}}\mathbf{Orb}
.

Definition 3.13
.

We call
W
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
locally finite if it is a retract of a locally finite
G
-
CW
-complex with finite stabilizers.

The following is an immediate consequence of
[
BELa
, Thm. 1.5]
and the definitions.

Corollary 3.14
.

If
W
is locally finite, then

p^{G,\mathrm{an},\mathrm{lf}}:K^{G,{\mathcal{X}},\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}(W)\to K^{G,\mathrm{an},\mathrm{lf}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(W))

is an equivalence.

Corollary 3.15
.

If
Y
is locally finite, then we have a commutative square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 123.77846pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-45.67526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.36606pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eqref{fiwqehiudhqwidqwedqwedqd}\circ T^{\mathrm{mot}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.96112pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.18776pt\raise 6.72002pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.71446pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{X}}$}}}\kern 75.58156pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Sigma K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}(\partial_{h}X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma K^{G,\mathrm{an}}_{{\mathbf{C}},A}(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(Y))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

3.5
The topological Paschke map

Let
E:G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be a weak equivariant Borel-Moore homology theory (
2.95
). We will construct the topologial Paschke morphism
as a natural transformation

p^{G,\mathrm{top}}:E\mathbf{P}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\to E\circ\iota_{u}^{\mathrm{top}}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}\ .

(3.17)

It is an analogue of the analytic Paschke morphism (
3.13
).

Remark 3.16
.

The domain of the analytic Paschke morphism (
3.13
) is an equivariant local homology theory
on
G\mathbf{UBC}
, i.e., homotopy invariant, excisive,
u
-continuous and trivial on flasques, see
3.2
.
This follows from (the equivariant version of)
[
BE20a
, Lem. 9.6]
since
K{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{{\mathbf{C}},A,G_{can,max}}
is a strong coarse homology theory. In contrast, the equivariant coarse homology theory
E\mathbf{P}
is not known to be strong.
Therefore the domain of the topological Paschke morphism
E\mathbf{P}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}
is not known to be a local homology theory since it may not annihilate flasques.
∎

We start with describing the transformation (
1.12
)

\pi:\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\to\iota_{u}^{\mathrm{top}}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\ .

(3.18)

in detail.
Its component at
Y
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
is the morphism

\pi_{Y}:\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)\to\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(Y)

(3.19)

defined as the Gelfand dual of the homomorphism of unital commutative
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras

C_{u,0}(Y)\to\frac{\ell_{{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)}}^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))}{\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)})}=C(\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\ ,

(3.20)

which
sends a function
\phi
in
C_{u,0}(Y)
to the class of the function

\tilde{\phi}:{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)\to{\mathbb{C}}\ ,\qquad(t,y)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\phi(y)&t\geq 0\\
0&t<0\end{array}\right.

(3.21)

in
\ell_{{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)}}^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))
.
The decay of the variation of
\tilde{\phi}

away from bounded subsets of the cone is implied by the decay of
\phi
outside of bounded subsets of
Y

together with the uniform continuity of
\phi
and the characterization of the coarse entourages of the cone for large times, see
Section

3.1
and
[
BELa
, Lem. 5.7]
. One thus checks that
(
3.20
) is well-defined and
that
\pi:=(\pi_{Y})_{Y\in G\mathbf{UBC}}
is a natural transformation.

Note that the component
\pi_{Y}
is precisely the map (
3.12
).

Precomposing the topological transgression from (
2.163
) with the functor
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}

we get the natural transformation

T^{G,\mathrm{top}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:E\mathbf{P}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\to\Sigma E\circ\partial_{h}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}\ .

(3.22)

Definition 3.17
.

We define the topological Paschke morphism as the composition

p^{G,\mathrm{top}}:E\mathbf{P}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\xrightarrow{T^{G,\mathrm{top}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty},\eqref{frfwrwerfwerfwer}}\Sigma E\circ\partial_{h}\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\xrightarrow{\Sigma E\pi,\eqref{gwreggjweiog}}\Sigma E\circ\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}

(3.23)

Note that
E\mathbf{P}:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is an equivariant coarse homology theory by
2.133
.
We now assume that the motivic transgression
T^{\mathrm{mot}}:\mathrm{Yo}(X)\to\mathrm{Yo}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))
from
3.9
is defined. Recall
k
from (
3.11
).

Theorem 3.18
.

The following square commutes:

\textstyle{E\mathbf{P}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{\mathrm{mot}}}
\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{top}}}
\textstyle{E\mathbf{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces)}
\scriptstyle{p^{G,\mathrm{top}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma E(\partial_{h}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{\Sigma E(\iota_{u}^{\mathrm{top}}(Y))}

Proof.

We expand the square as follows:

\textstyle{E\mathbf{P}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{top}}}
\textstyle{E\mathbf{P}(\hat{X}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{top}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces E\mathbf{P}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{G,\mathrm{top}}}
\scriptstyle{\eqref{gwerfwerfw3fweferfwer},j}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{p^{G,\mathrm{top}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Sigma E(\partial_{h}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{h}\psi}
\textstyle{\Sigma E(\partial_{h}\hat{X}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(
3.10
)
\textstyle{\Sigma E(\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\Sigma E(\iota_{u}^{\mathrm{top}}(Y))}

The arrow labeled by (
3.10
) is the Gelfand dual of

C_{u,0}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y))\to\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\hat{X}^{\prime}}}(\hat{X}^{\prime})\to\frac{\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{B}}_{\hat{X}}}(\hat{X}^{\prime})}{\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}}_{\hat{X}^{\prime}})}\ ,

where the first map in the lower line is given by (
3.21
) and
the second map is extension by zero.
The left lower triangle commutes by the definition (
3.11
) of
k
, and the commutativity of the right lower triangle can easily be checked on the level of Gelfand dual algebras.
∎

3.6
Higson-dominated compactifications

We consider a bornological coarse space
X
.
Let
A
be any closed unital subalgebra of
\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(X)

containing
\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})
(see (
2.1
) and (
2.2
)).
By Gelfand duality it determines
a compact Hausdorff space
\bar{X}
such that
C(\bar{X})=A
.
We can consider
\bar{X}
as a compactification of the discrete space
X
.
The Gelfand dual of the right vertical map in the following map of exact sequences of commutative
C^{*}
-algebras

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A/\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(\partial_{h}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is a map of compact Hausdorff spaces
\pi:\partial_{h}X\to Y
where
Y
is determined by
C(Y)=A/\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})
.

Definition 3.19
.

We call
\bar{X}
a Higson-dominated compactification of
X
with boundary
Y
.

We can identify the set
X
considered as a discrete topological spaces with the Gelfand dual of
\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}}_{min})
, where
{\mathcal{B}}_{min}
is the minimal bornology on
X
consisting of finite subsets.
We have a natural map

\bar{X}\supseteq X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}_{X}}}{{\to}}X

in
\mathbf{LCH}^{+}
corresponding via Gelfand duality to the
inclusion
\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}}_{min})\to\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})\to A
.
We let
p:X\to\bar{X}
be the corresponding open inclusion map.

Assumption 3.20
.

We assume the following data to be given:

1.

a neighbourhood
U
of
Y
in
\bar{X}
and a retraction
r:U\to Y
.

2.

a proper, bornological and controlled function
s:X\to[0,\infty)
.

3.

a point
y_{0}
in
Y
.

We define a map of sets

\psi:X\to[0,\infty)\times Y\ ,\quad\psi(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(s(x),y_{0})&x\not\in p^{-1}(U)\\
(s(x),r(p(x)))&x\in p^{-1}(U)\end{array}\right.

(3.24)

Since
Y
is a compact Hausdorff space it has a canonical uniform bornological coarse structure.
By definition its bornological and coarse structures are the maximal ones. The uniform structure consists of all neighbourhoods of the diagonal.
Using this uniform bornological coarse structure we equip
[0,\infty)\times Y
with the bornological coarse structure of the cone
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
, see (
3.1
) and the text before that.

Lemma 3.21
.

The map
\psi:X\to{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
in (
3.24
) is a morphism of bornological coarse spaces.

Proof.

First of all, any bounded subset of
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
is contained in a set of the form
[0,R]\times Y
for some
R
in
[0,\infty)
. Then
\psi^{-1}([0,R]\times Y)\subseteq s^{-1}([0,R])
and is therefore bounded since
s
is proper.
This shows that
\psi
is proper.

Let
W
be any entourage of
X
.
Since
s
is controlled
we have

\sup_{(x,x^{\prime})\in W}|s(x)-s(x^{\prime})|=:C<\infty\ .

(3.25)

This shows that the first component of
\psi
is controlled.

The subset
X\setminus p^{-1}(U)
is a bounded subset of
X
. Indeed, since
\bar{X}
is compact Hausdorff and hence normal,
by Tietze’s extension theorem there exist a function
f
in
C(\bar{X})
such that

f_{|\bar{X}\setminus U}=1
and
f_{|Y}=0
. We then have
f\in{\mathtt{ker}}(C(\bar{X})\to C(Y))=\ell^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})
.
This implies
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\|f_{|X\setminus B}\|=0
. In particular there exists
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}
such that

X\setminus p^{-1}(U)\subseteq B
.

Note that since
s
is bornological the set

s(X\setminus p^{-1}(U))
is bounded in
[0,\infty)
.

Since
Y
has the maximal coarse structure,
it remains to show that for every neighbourhood
V
of the diagonal of
Y
there exists

R
in
[0,\infty)
such that
(x,x^{\prime})\in W
and

s(x)\geq R
implies
(r(p(x)),r(p(x^{\prime})))\in V
. Here we take
R
so large that
s(X\setminus p^{-1}(U))\subseteq[0,R-C)
.
Then
s(x^{\prime})\geq R-C
by (
3.25
) and therefore

x,x^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(U)
and
r(p(x)),r(p(x^{\prime}))
in
Y
are defined.

Assume the contrary. Then there exists a neighbourhood
V
of the diagonal of
Y
and a sequence

((x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}))_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
in
W
with
s(x_{i})\to\infty
and

(r(p(x_{i})),r(p(x_{i}^{\prime})))\not\in V
.
By compactness of
Y
we can assume that
r^{\prime}(p(x_{i}))\to z
and
r^{\prime}(p(x_{i}^{\prime}))\to z^{\prime}
.
Then
z^{\prime}\not=z
.

Since
\bar{X}
is normal as observed above, Tietze’s extension theorem gives a continuous function
f:\bar{X}\to[0,1]
such that
f(z)=0
and
f(z^{\prime})=1
. Since
\bar{X}
is a Higson-dominated compactification
we have
f_{|X}\in\ell^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(X)
. Since
s
is proper there exists

S
in
[0,\infty)
such that
s(x)\geq S
and
(x,x^{\prime})\in W
implies that
|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|\leq 1/2
.

In particular,
|f(x_{i})-f(x_{i}^{\prime})|\leq 1/2
for all
i
in
{\rm Nature}
such that
s(x_{i})\geq S
.
Taking the limit over
i
in
{\rm Nature}
we conclude
|f(z)-f(z^{\prime})|\leq 1/2
. This a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 3.22
.

If
X
is a bornological coarse space and
\bar{X}
is a Higson-dominated compactification of
X
with boundary
Y

with the data
3.20
, then the motivic transgression
T^{\mathrm{mot}}
is defined (with
X^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(Y)
and the canonical map
\pi:\partial_{h}X\to Y
).

Let
\bar{X}
be a Higson-dominated compactification of
X
with boundary
Y
.

If the bornological coarse space
X
is presented by a metric
d
and non-empty, then
we can choose a base point
x_{0}
and set

s(-):=d(x_{0},-):X\to[0,\infty)\ .

This function is proper, bornological and controlled.

Assume that
\bar{X}
is metrizable and that
Y
is an absolute neighbourhood retract.
Then there exists a neighborhood
U
of
Y
in
\bar{X}
and a retraction
r:U\to Y
.

Corollary 3.23
.

If
X
is a metrizable bornological coarse space and
\bar{X}
is a metrizable Higson-dominated compactification of
X
with boundary
Y
such that
Y
is an absolute neighbourhood retract, then the motivic transgression
T^{\mathrm{mot}}
is defined (with
X^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(Y)
and the canonical map
\pi:\partial_{h}X\to Y
).

Note that there is now relation between the metrics of
X
and
\bar{X}
are required.

Note that finite CW-complexes are absolute neighbourhood retracts.

Corollary 3.24
.

If
X
is a metrizable bornological coarse space and
\bar{X}
is a metrizable Higson-dominated compactification of
X
by a finite CW-complex
Y
, then the motivic transgression
T^{\mathrm{mot}}
is defined (with
X^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(Y)
and the canonical map
\pi:\partial_{h}X\to Y
).

3.7
Extending commutativity using homotopy theory

In this short section we explain how to extend morphisms between natural transformations.
This is used in
Section

1.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{C}}
be cocomplete
\infty
-categories,

E,F:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be colimit-preserving functors, and
\phi,\psi:E\to F
be natural transformations.
Let
f:{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
be some functor and assume that

f^{*}\phi\simeq f^{*}\psi\ .

(3.26)

We form the left-Kan extension

\textstyle{{\mathcal{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{PSh}}({\mathcal{G}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{f}}

where
\hat{f}
preserves colimits.
By the universal property of presheaves we have an equivalence

y^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}({\mathbf{PSh}}({\mathcal{G}}),{\mathcal{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{C}})\ .

The equivalence

y^{*}\hat{f}^{*}\phi\simeq f^{*}\phi\simeq f^{*}\psi\simeq y^{*}\hat{f}^{*}\psi

therefore induces an equivalence

\hat{f}^{*}\phi\simeq\hat{f}^{*}\psi
.
In particular, for every presheaf
Y
in
{\mathbf{PSh}}({\mathcal{G}})
we have an equivalence

\phi_{\hat{f}(Y)}\simeq\psi_{\hat{f}(Y)}:E(\hat{f}(Y))\to F(\hat{f}(Y))\ .

We conclude:

Corollary 3.25
.

For every object
D
in the essential image of
\hat{f}
we have an equivalence

\hat{\phi}_{D}\simeq\hat{\psi}_{D}:E(D)\to F(D)

of morphisms in
{\mathcal{C}}
.

The equivalence (
3.26
) of natural transformations therefore extends to an objectwise
equivalence on the essential image of
\hat{f}
. Note that
this argument does not give an equivalence of natural transformations
\hat{f}^{*}\phi
and
\hat{f}^{*}\psi
.

Example 3.26
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{C}}=\mathbf{Spc}
or
{\mathcal{C}}=\mathbf{Sp}
. The existence of the equivalence

\hat{\phi}_{D}\simeq\hat{\psi}_{D}
implies that

\phi_{D,*}=\psi_{D,*}:\pi_{*}E(D)\to\pi_{*}F(D)
.
We therefore get an equality of natural transformations between the functors
\pi_{*}E
and
\pi_{*}F

on the essential image of
\hat{D}
.
∎

If one needs at least some spectrum-valued naturality one can use the following.
Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be some
\infty
-category and consider the bold part of
the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern Corollary 3.27
.

When
i
admits a lift
\hat{i}
, then we have an equivalence
i^{*}E\simeq i^{*}F
.

3.8
Normal equivariant homology theories

In
[
BE20a
]
we introduced the notion of a local homology theory.
In the reference the axioms were chosen so that the coarsification of a local homology theory is
a coarse homology theory and that the composition
of the cone functor
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{BC}
with a strong equivariant coarse homology theory is an equivariant local homology theory, see also
3.2
. These sidebars leave some freedom for the choice of the excision axiom.
In the present paper we fix the excision axiom such that pull-backs of weak equivariant Borel-Moore
homology theories via
\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}:G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}
are excisive.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}
and
A
be an invariant closed subset.

Definition 3.28
.

We call
A
normal if the restriction
C_{0,u}(X)\to C_{0,u}(A)
is surjective.

We refer to
Section

2.3
for the notation.

We consider a functor
E:G\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
to a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category.
In analogy to
[
BE20a
, Def. 3.12]
we adopt the following definition.

Definition 3.29
.

E
is called a normal equivariant
local homology theory if it

1.

is homotopy invariant,

2.

is excisive for equivariant coarsely and uniformly excisive decompositions into normal closed subspaces,

3.

is
u
-continuous,

4.

vanishes on flasques.

Here normal refers to the additional normality condition in
Item

2
.

Example 3.30
.

If
X
is a
G
-simplicial complex with the spherical path metric and
A
is an invariant subcomplex,
then
A
is a normal subset. This is all what is needed to conclude that
the coarsification
2.125
of a normal equivariant local homology theory is a coarse homology theory.

∎

As in
[
BE20a
, Sec. 4]
we can construct
the universal normal equivariant local homology theory

\mathrm{Yo}\mathcal{N}:G\mathbf{UBC}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}(G\mathbf{UBC},\mathbf{Sp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\to}}G\mathbf{Sp}\mathcal{N}

where
y
is the Yoneda embedding and
L
is a left Bousfield localization
which precisely forces that
\mathrm{Yo}\mathcal{N}
satisfies the axioms listed in
3.29
.
Note that
G\mathbf{Sp}\mathcal{N}
is a presentable stable
\infty
-category and for every cocomplete stable
\infty
-category

{\mathcal{C}}
we have an equivalence

\mathrm{Yo}\mathcal{N}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(G\mathbf{Sp}\mathcal{N},{\mathcal{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{\mathrm{nloc}}}(G\mathbf{UBC},{\mathcal{C}})

where the superscript
\mathrm{nloc}
stands for the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Fun}}(G\mathbf{UBC},{\mathcal{C}})
of normal equivariant local homology theories.

Example 3.31
.

Here are our main examples of normal equivariant local homology theories.

1.

If
E:G\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is a strong equivariant coarse homology theory, then the composition

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:G\mathbf{UBC}\xrightarrow{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}}G\mathbf{BC}\xrightarrow{E}{\mathcal{C}}

is a normal equivariant local homology theory (the proof is analogous to the non-equivariant case
[
BE20a
, Lem. 9.6]
).

2.

If
E:G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is an equivariant weak Borel-Moore homology theory, then the composition

E\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}:G\mathbf{UBC}\xrightarrow{\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}}G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}\xrightarrow{E}{\mathcal{C}}

is a normal equivariant local homology theory. The functor is obviously
u
-continuous.
The functor

\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}
preserves homotopies, flasques and sends equivariant coarsely and uniformly excisive decompositions into normal closed subspaces to invariant closed decompositions. We then
use that
E
is homotopy invariant, vanishes on flasques in
G\mathbf{LCH}^{+}
, and is strongly excisive in order to verify the conditions from
3.29
.

∎

Evaluating the diagram (
1.10
)
at
X={\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Z)
and the canonical map

\pi:\partial_{h}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Z)\to\iota^{\mathrm{top}}_{u}(Z)

from (
1.12
) for
Z
in
G\mathbf{UBC}

we get the diagram
(
1.13
).
